Setting up the annual budget, authorizing staff rhanges and appointments, approving all leases and contracts—these ojxTulions are part of the work done for Purdue University by its Board of Trustees. Composing the board are two agriculturalists, two industrialists, two citizens of character and distinction ' and three alumni members. Each member is appointed by the Governor for a three year term. Of these nine trustees, seven on this year’s board are Purdue graduates. In the past year, the lmard has authorized the sites for the new basketball arena and the new Pharmacy Building, a four-year program at the Calumet and Fl Wayne Campuses, and an inc tease in residence hall rates. 78 Board of Trustees r Working toward the Purdue University of 1080, President Frederick I.. Hovde sets the | olicies today that will enable his vision of Purdue ten, twenty, even thirty years from now to become a reality. A campus of thirty thousand graduates and undergrads led from a vast net of extensions—this is the Purdue that the president works with every day. Through the weekly routine of meetings, conferences. and administrative details. President Hovde is guiding Purdue into the future toward an idea that is to many obscure, but is to him as near as the next U-hall. 81 Ninety percent of a student's college career is spent outside the classroom under the auspices of Dr. Donald R. Mallett. Vice President and r.xecutive Dean. From the Office ol Admissions to the ceremonies at commencement. Dean Mallett is resjx� nsil� lc for every facet of student life. The reputation of Purdue—the education of its students is in the hands of Dr. Paul F. Chenea, Vice President for Academic Affairs. In the planning, organization, and development of the academic program of the university, Dr. Chenea is setting the standards that make Purdue's graduates some of the most sought after in the nation. 82 Vice Presidents Everything from the financing of a multi-million dollar residence hall to the requisitioning of a lenient box of paper clips is authorized by Mr. Lytle J. Kreehafer, Vice President and Treasurer of the University. He is responsible for the management of all funds for such things as the Physical Plant, Airport, and Residences. With the Graduate School comprising one-fourth of the University, Dr. Frederick N. Andrews, coordinates many interrelated and overlapping areas of research and graduate study. In the dual capacity of Vice President for Research and Dean of the Graduate School, Dr. Andrews asMsts departments and students doing research by securing support from private and governmental agencies. 83 Deans and Directors NELSON PARKHURST Registrar O. HAWKINS Dean of University Extension Dean of Engineering 85 Calumet Serving as a jxrtpourri of culture, the Calumet Campus provides special educational opportunities for tlic diverse population of the greater Chicago area. Closely linked with several prominent steel corporations, the Calumet regional Campus furnishes training and manpower needed by industry, as well as excellent career foundations. Fort Wayne Through the efforts of the Purdue and Indiana University faculties, the regional Campus at Fort Wayne offers many cultural advantages. A Shakespeare Week program, a new building dedicated for research, and Christmas parties are only a few of the projects sponsored through the combined talents of double administration. In addition, students at Fort Wayne enjoy such activities as a glee club, newspaper, student assembly, and intramural sports —functions not commonly found on an extension campus. Sharing facilities with I.U. provides these chances. 87 Michigan City Ground-breaking ceremonies in the fall marked the beginning of construction of the new North Central Regional Campus that is scheduled to open for the 1967 school year. Replacing the present center at Michigan City, the new campus, located in West- ilie. will serve the tri-city area of LaPorte, Valparaiso, and Michigan City. The problems of making the transition from the elegant John Barker mansion to the Wcstvillc complex will be compounded by an overnight doubling of the enrollment. 88 Indianapolis JACK M. RYDER Director Family responsibilities and lull-time jobs are no obstacle to people still interested in obtaining a college education. The Indianapolis Campus has an extensive night school program, as well as undergraduate curricula for students planning to obtain their B.S. degree elsewhere. With an enrollment of nearly three thousand, this regional campus is playing a major role in the education of Hoosiers. 89 W. F. HENTSCHEL Treasurer Purdue Research Foundation Serving as the corporate arm of the I'Diversity, Purdue Research Foundation not only grants fellowships sponsored by industry and governmental agencies, but finances University and community development. McClure Park, the Foundations's research complex, offers industry an op|x rtunity to apply the spin-olf from basic discoveries to marketable products. PRF itself also sponsors grants and fellowships to assist graduate students and staff in their research. In aiding the development of the University, PRF funds the constitution necessary to keep the Physical Plant growing and also finances student and staff facilities. Tower Acres, Purdue Service Center, as well as several fraternity and sorority houses have been built with the Foundation's assistance. For the community, PRF has donated park and playground areas. 00 Alumni Association E. B. BAUGH, Manager of Public Relations: R. J. RUDOLPH. Executive Secretary; C. H. HALL, Executive Director. Promoting interest and informing graduates is accomplished by the Purdue Alumni Association with the publication of its monthly magazine. The Purdue Alumnus. The Association is responsible for reuniting Purdue graduates through the regular meetings of its over one hundred Alumni Clubs. Former students from all over the country are brought together by the Association at such special activities as Gala Week, held each spring, and the traditional Homecoming, an event in the fall. One of the largest parts of the Alumni Association at the present time is the Purdue Alumni Scholarship Foundation. This Foundation, created by the Alumni Assoc iation. accepts gifts and donations and awards them to deserving students of the University. In the past year, the Scholarship Foundation awarded close to $250,000 in more than eleven hundred scholarships and grants. Among the projects supported by the Alumni Scholarship Foundation are the Graduate Fellowship Fund and the Athletic Grant-in-Aid Fund. UNDERSTAND NEEDS AND FILL THEM. 9(� 97 08 I ' PEI 1 R W. MCFADDEN Dean of Mechanical Engineering ALBER'I R. SPALDING Dean of Freshman Engineering Engineering Making an idea a piece of hardware, transform ing a need into a product, the engineer explores imagination for a practical approach. Synthesis and solution are his goals: creativity and patience his assets; mathematics and science his tools. Basically a pragmatist, the engineer delves not only for new products, but also for new ways to make old ones. Every achievement, whether a better can opener or a Saturn rocket, means advancement for a society started and perpetuated by the engineer. JOHN C. HANCOCK Dean of Moccrif-il Engineering 101 HAROLD T. AMRINE Dean of Industrial Engineering GERALD V. ISAACS Dean of Vgrirultutal Engineering 102 Bui the knowledge to enter the rapidly changing profession of engineering cannot he obtained overnight. The pace of the art itself dictates the type of education that must he offered. Advancing technology requires that the graduating engineer take with hint not only the techniques and methods of the day but also a background firmly rooted in basic theory so that as new approaches and methods of solution to problems are developed, the Purdue engi ncer will he able to incorjH� ratc them into the project of the moment and the projects of tomorrow. 103 To give the engineer this capacity lor expanding knowledge, all engineers begin as one in Freshman Engineering. After a year of basic physics, math, chemistry, mechanics, and graphics, the engineers are divided into specialized disciplines. Even then the emphasis is placed on the theoretical and general with laboratories and specialized courses interjected to give the engineer skill at applying the theoretical to the practical. And finally, as graduation approaches, the engineer is offered two alternatives: one that continues stressing the abstract and mathematical approach in preparation for graduate school; or one that concentrates almost entirely on problem solution and synthesis needed to enter the business world. br age r.oi.niNc; Dean of Chemical Engineering 105 For ihe nine-to-five people of business, dinner signals the end of the day: for the engineering student, dinner is only a pause in a day much longer. In a world of integrals and circuits, coffee, cigarettes, and a slidcrule become nightly companions. Lab reports drag into volumes of graphs and calculations stealing hours of sleep. Formulas and processes memorized and regurgitated for a grade often seem pointless in a pragmatic sense. Yet. the graduate seldom feels that he is unprepared for his job or that his knowledge is useless. Sometime before the last all-nighter, the student becomes an engi necr. The coffee and the slidcrule do not disappear, but the pointless feeling does—'business calls. GERALD A. LEONARDS Dean of Civil Engineering 106 107 Humanities, Social Science, and Education Listening to tapes in the A-V Center, scrambling to make a twelve o’clock badminton class, preparing a lesson for a class of eight year-olds— these arc all a part of the life of a student in the School of Humanities, Social Science, and Education, Purdue's newest school. Since its organization in 1903, HSSE has grown twice as fast as the rest of the university and is now the second largest school with an enrollment of over 3000. Students in this school may choose from any of fourteen major areas, either teaching or non-teaching, to receive a B.A., B.S., or B.P.E. degree. Special features of HSSE include a counseling service, an honors program, and foreign study abroad. MAKBI RV B. OGLE. JR. Dean of Humanities, Soria! Science, and Education 108 109 Paralleling its rapid growth of the last few year . HSSE has initiated new programs and projects in many of its departments. Being organized now in the Art department is a 5-year undergraduate program in industrial design. Sociologists are involved in a major research problem in the Calumet region—a “living lab on urban problems. An ex pcriniciu in the Foreign Language department, and one unique to Purdue, is the transmitting of the 101 and 102 tapes to McCutcheon Hall. In addition, the mIiooI offers several special services, including a •Speech and Hearing Clinic, marriage counseling, and psychological services. Ill Agriculture and Forestry “I'm majoring in Turf. To most Purdue students this statement would probably mean the speaker drank more beer than the other guys. To an Ag student though, getting a major in Turf means preparing for a career in high quality grass growing. Besides the Turf major, the School of Agriculture provides instruction in everything from horticulture to biochemistry. With its thousands of acres of farmland and hundreds of animals, the Ag School also maintains laboratories such as the Poultry Science Building which contains incubators for 25.000 eggs. With instructors and researchers in the classroom and cattle barn the Ag School educates its students and benefits the whole country. 112 113 114 r I Whack! Bam! Crash! No, it’s not Batman; it’s •i Purdue forester winning a log-chopping contest during the annual forestry field day. Even more important to forestry students titan sawing down trees are such tilings as silviculture and forest surveying. Besides regular field trips to woods and forests owned by the university, students, instructors, and members of the research staff work in national and state parks during the summer months to help conserve the nation’s national resources. 115 Science MO At the present time, Purdue exceeds all other American universities with its thirty-thousand science students. Dean Felix Haas says, “Purdue has one of the largest training programs in the world for the development of future scientists. To accommodate this great number, the science school staff consists of about thirteen-hundred jx'ople teaciting everything from differential equations to stellar astronomy. While the main job of the faculty is to teach and do research, the administration’s goal is to attract the most talented jxrople to this campus and to keep them here. Believing that the quality of the teaching is determined by the quality of the faculty, the administration thus always trys to improve the actual teaching process. FELIX HAAS Dean of Science 117 Bubble, bubble, toil and trouble,” That’s what some chemistry labs arc made of. Not all labs arc like that of course: some are much Gilmer. In many biology labs for instance, students must use patience and tender loving care” to grow beans and other plants. Besides chemistry and biology, the science school includes physics, statistics, computer sciences. and mathematics. Along with the regular program of these six departments, the school offers special facilities, such as the computer center, which students may use free of charge. The science faculty does research in many fields. Thus the school serves the student and the whole country. 118 119 Industrial Management Linking the rapidly changing technologies 1 the scientific laboratory to the needs of the people, the industrial manager must he able to solve the problems posed by the technological developments, and then exploit its opportunities. Designed to prepare its graduates for this role in industry, the Industrial Management School’s curriculum provides for the development and understanding of useful managerial skills in all the major areas of IM. Theory and applied courses are effectively integrated so that the student can test his knowledge of the subject in the classroom. This technique is especially useful in Collective Bargaining where the class is divided into labor and management to attempt the settlement of actual problems. Tailored to meet the needs of tlie school are the facilities of the new Krannert Building dedicated by Mr. and Mrs. Merman C. Krannert in May, 1965. The Krennert School’s library alone occupies two floors of the building and contains 25,000 volumes. Another entire floor is equipped with specialized research equipment including one of the nation’s most modern laboratories for the study of behavior in administrative situations. The lower level of the Krannert Building is occupied by classrooms specifically designed for maximum student participation. To organize and direct the discussions is a distinguished faculty whose members have international reputations in their respective fields. 123 Home Economics, besides preparing husband-seeking coeds for many future years of housekeeping, provides them with an exciting variety of careers. Leading them into the fields of Textile Technology, Restaurant-Institutional Management, Housing and Equipment, and Nursery Kindergarten, the Home Ec. School stresses more common c lasses for thought, such as. Fashion Retailing, Interior Design, Dietetics, Foods in Business or Nutrition Research. and Home Economics Teaching. Students of Home Ec. may be found struggling in foods or textile labs, chasing after youngsters in the CDFL building, bustling about in the Home Ec. c afeteria, or c almly sketching in an art class. Home Economics 125 Pharmacy Prospects of a new building, with expanded research and undergraduate teaching facilities, await students in the School of Pharmacy and Pliarmacal Sciences. At present, Purdue's pharmacy school lias the largest graduate enrollment in the country, in addition to having one of the largest percentages of undergraduate women. After a prepharmacy program of two years in the School of Science, students are eligible for admission to the School of Pharmacy where they are trained to be retail or professional druggists, hospital pharmacists, pharmaceutical chemists, or pharmacologists. CIlliCAL VOI 126 127 Since its organization in July, 1964, the School of Technology lias become the third largest school in the university, with a large part of its enrollment and strength on the four regional campuses. The basis for the curricula of this new school was that many courses, while no longer necessary to the engineer, are useful in today’s technological society and were incorporated into the School of Technology. Unique to this school is the Associate of Applied Science degree, granted after two years of specialized college-level training. Upon graduation, these technicians are immediately employable in industry. The school also offers a four-year program leading to a B.S. in Industrial Education, and many of its graduates elect to pursue an M.S. or Ph.I)., also granted by the School of Technology. 128 School of Technology 129 WELLMAN L. FRANCE Associate Head PF.M Demonstrating a prowess not only in athletics hut in sportscasting, business, or athletic training, more than 210 undergraduates and 100 graduates partic ipate in Purdue's program of Physical Keltic a lion for Men. Although only one of the departments in the school of Humanities. Social Science, and Kducaiion, PKM offers its own degree. Those majoring in health education, and recreational education receive the 11. A. degree, while the Bachelor ol Physical Kducation is awarded to physical educa lion majors. Divided into four disciplines, teaching, coaching, athletic training and lion-teaching option with area of interest, the school of Physical Kducation prepares its graduates to assume a responsible role in a modern Society. 131 132 Veterinary Science Training men and women to promote (lie wellbeing of man as he is affected by his relationships to animals is the ultimate aim of the School of Veterinary Science and Medicine. To accomplish this goal, the school first provides professional training for those aspiring to become veterinarians, and second, offers courses for a bettci understanding of animal health problems. After a two-year pre-veterinary program administered by the School of Agriculture. Vet students turn to the study of animal anatomy, physiology, microbiology, pathology, and pharmacology for two years. The last part of their six years of study is concerned with the application of these sciences to therapeutics and medicine. In addition to their professional training. Vet students learn much about the nature and control of animal diseases through their research programs, conducted at the Veterinary Science Research Farm. Delta Rho Kappa Molding an initiation banquet each fall for its new members, Delta Rho Kappa, the honorary for students in the Schools of Humanities and Science, begins the year. Requirements for this organization range from the upper two percent of the class fot a Junior f� to the top ten percent for a Senior. Besides recognizing high scholastic achievement, Delta Rho Kappa endorses honors meetings for high ranking students in the freshman class to explain and encourage honors courses and graduate study. Rho Chi 135 Tau Beta Pi Since the founding of Purdue’s chapter of Tail Beta Pi. national all-engineering honorary, it lias become the largest in the country. Pledges of this organization must meet stiff academic requirements, ranging from a 5.05 graduation index for a Senior 8 to a 5.5 index for a Junior 5. Phi Eta Sigma Students seen on campus carrying scroll-shaped pledge boards are probably pledges of Phi F.ta Sigma, national freshman men's scholastic honorary. Members of this honorary are those men who have a 5.5 index for their first semester or a 5.5 accumulative index for their first year. Purdue's chapter of Phi Eta Sigma now ranks third nationally in membership, and “at its present rate of growth, it may soon be first. Besides recognizing superior freshmen. Alpha Lambda Delta (� ood morning and congratulations! were the words heard by 117 sleepy freshman women as they were awakened early in the morning and tapped for membership in Alpha lambda Delta, national scholastic honorary for freshman women. High scholastic achievement and the development of leadership arc the key phrases for this organization and are exempli lied by the honorary's colors: red for knowledge, white for purity, and gold for honor. To be eligible for membership, freshmen must have either a 5.5 index for the first semester or a 5.5 accumulative index after two semesters, and must have carried at least fourteen hours. The outstanding feature of the year was a spring banquet with Phi Ida Sigma. 141 From the huge shining star at the top at Christmas time to the coed laundry room in the basement, the Graduate House and its people live quite differently from the rest of the campus. When most undergraduates stop work near midnight, graduates students work out plans for more experiments. type thesis, or pace their carpeted hallways smoking and thinking of all that must be done-everything that must l e crammed in before the goal of all the years of study is reached. At other times, grad students may be found in a tiny lab somewhere running tests and more tests or in a classroom teaching freshmen who either are petrified or know it all already. Maybe, they'll be at someone's apartment eating a non I'nion meal or just “with the guys at Harry's. Some Monday mornings after a long weekend the graduate student may think h will never survive the next series of experiments, the next set of tests that must be graded: but some Iioh he pulls through and struggles on toward that always glowing prize, the Masters or Doctorate. 142 Graduate students arc counseled and direc ted through their final years of schooling by depatt-mcntal counselors, major professors, and the deans of the graduate school. The administrative olfico controls admissions and formulates graduate school policy by the decisions of its Graduate Council, which is composed of representatives from the departments on campus. Assistant deans of the school are appointed from the faculty in different fields of study for three year periods so that they may better understand how the school is operated and may gain experience in administration. These deans coordinate research sponsorship, graduate student plans of study, and fellowship administration. Since the various departments along with their research and teaching staffs arc spread throughout the campus, the graduate school administration is “central office for a very decentralized area.” Ml V. E. Pietsdi. T. Platt, P. Howard. R. Hynds. R. Kochcrt. G. Lahr, P. Alpha Phi Omega Promoting service, leadership, and fellowship unite the men of Alpha Phi Omega in a spirit of accomplishment. Keeping the campus bulletin boards up to date, being responsible for administering campus elections, holding Christmas parties, giving swimming lessons, and helping with the publication of campus maps are characteristic of the services they render. Established as The National Service Fraternity, it is the largest Greek letter service organization in the world. Along with this distinction. Alpha Phi Omega is proud of its pledge program. which begins with an unexpected midnight tapping ceremony. Even with all these activities, the men of Alpha Phi Omega manage to keep their grades well above average. Wankat. Front Row: P. Christenson. C. Nagel, E. Schmidt. R. Boyd. M. Boas. M. Rcvis, W. Student Court Student court performs an important campus function by providing student decisions on student actions. Operating as the judicial branch of Purdue Student Government, Student Court is composed of a Chief Justice, associate justices, investigators, and secretaries. The Court reviews and tries student traffic and passport violations and other disciplinary cases involving students and student organizations. In the future it is hoped that Student Court can become the Supreme Court for campus affairs where cases may be appealed from separate housing unit judicial groups. 175 SKNIOR CLASS COUNCI 1.- W Row: J. These informal dances helped round out the schedule which included such special events as Homecoming, Senior Cord Day, and Dad's Day. Another cultural effort on the part of Student Union consists of the well-known art displays brought to Purdue for the community as well as the campus. Christinas Around the World was another drawing card for local residents, while the Christmas Decorating Party helped spread holiday cheer campus-wide. 185 Noise and confusion continually rise from the depths of the Memorial Union and the Student Union workshop. Hammers pounding, saws buzzing, or maybe just the slap of a paint brush can be heard above the busy hum as workers prepare posters and backdrops for their own and other campus events. Other forms ol publicity also originate here as Student Union continues to work to keep the campus alive and well-informed. Neubaucr. 187 Debris Typewriters clattering, flashbulbs popping, and ideas flying . . . These three arc the essential and obvious ingredients for any yearbook. Nevertheless, the backstage work involved in producing the Debris is overwhelming. Photographers snap pictures of every campus event from the excited crowds at the football games to a solitary student engrossed in thought, lint pictures must also be developed and proofed, printed and cropped before they become an actual part of the yearbook. Words, too, must be found to accompany these pictures and to recapture the mood of the moment. Typing and correcting and retyping must all enter in before the copy can be approved. Finally, the paragraphs and photographs are coordinated into a book—the Debris, an annual history of campus life at Purdue I'nivcrsity. DAVE DAY 1 ON Photography Editor 189 EDITORIAL STAFF JR. The staccato tapping of typewriters, the clatter of a teletype machine, the background chatter of voices—once a visitor enters the office of the Exponent, lie becomes aware of the tremendous responsibility of publishing any daily newspaper. Very shortly he realizes that the greatest proportion of this responsibility falls on the shoulders of the editorial staff—people charged with interpreting the news. By virtue of their positions, the editors decide what news within the campus and surrounding areas is to be reported and discussed in the paper. Decisions of policy by the editorial staff must always be made with foresight and celerity; for. like all newspapers, the Exponent faces a daily deadline. The editors consider 1900 a year of change, due to reorganization made during the previous semester and the addition of badly-needed equipment. The fresh format of this year’s Exponent was due primarily to the successful attempts of the editorial staff to improve the effectiveness of the paper. JOE SMITH Editor I SPOR TS STAFF - W. Lcvenfcld, J. Wade. J. Brod. DAN F.DMUNDSON City Editor Launching pad for every controversy on campus, the Exponent strives to awaken in each student an opinion. The Exponent massages the nerves of the campus to action offering the students an almost professional sports section, a pertinent selection of news events, and many editorials written by world newsmen. Reporters encounter persons in the sports and entertainment fields from across the country while serving the staff of the newspaper, and absorb information to transfer to Purdue. Working into the morning hours, the night crew remains dedicated to serving the 5,000 subscribers of the campus newspaper. Not every thought printed in the newspaper comes from the minds of eminent men, however, for the Exponent exists to speak the student's word, to shout his song into every ear. 190 r BOB PAI.AN PHOTO STAFF - J. Weller, CHARLES BREMER Captain POMM Membership in the Purdue Order of Military Merit is awarded to the most promising cadets in advanced Army ROTC in order to provide an incentive for continued accomplishments in the Army Corps. It offered the candidates alternate programs including the regular or scholarship program for men considering a Naval career, and a contract program which obligates candidates to three years service after graduation but does not give any financial assistance to their education. Both options introduce the Student to the responsibilities attached to being an officer in the U.S. Naval tradition. 231 Air Force ROTC September began the first year of voluntary Air Force ROTC at Purdue, and the initiation of a new option which leads to a commission in the USAF after two years of ROTC, instead of the customary lour. This new curriculum, designed to encourage military interest and incentive, brought over one-thousand cadets into the program. Another innovation this year is the quota being placed on juniors entering the advanced course. With this quota system. competition for commission in the U.S. Air Force will be much keener. 232 233 Arnold Air Society Social activities complementing service provide a diversified program for Purdue Arnold Air Society. Beating I.U., an annual habit for Purdue, climaxed the most sucessful season since 1943. GEORGE KING Basketball Coach Basketball He makes it look so easy! This was a familiar statement heard at a Purdue basketball game when Dave Sehellhase scored another two points while he was double teamed in every game. Many basketball experts across the nation feel that the All-American is the best that Purdue has ever seen. Sehellhase has broken every school scoring record imaginable in his brillant three year campaign with his “big game” coming when he poured in fifty-seven points against the big Ten champions, Michigan for the Big Ten single game scoring record. 256 The Purdues finished the season with a 4-10 Big Ten record and an 8-16 overall record. 1'his record does not tell the entire story however, as the Boilers lost many of their earlier games by as little as one point. Purdue showed the calibre of basketball it is capable of playing in the Los Angeles Classic by upsetting nationally ranked St. John's. Another bright spot of this year’s hardwood campaign came with a convincing victory over Indiana University which ran our win streak over the luckless 1 foosiers to two years in succession. 258 Many of the Riveters' early losses were l y close margins which indicates the potential that the team possesses. Sailing Club Tacking across the waters of Lake Shafer and dodging big Merc outboards, the sailing club enjoys the gusty winds that drive less hardy persons indoors. Of course, even these nautical-minded students have a limit. When the weather finally overpowers their enthusiasm, they return to jxut and thaw out over an oilburning stove. The coed club has about twelve boats available for member's use. Those with a skipper rating can captain their own boat: oilier members can sign on as crewmen. The c lub's purpose is to teach the techniques of sailing. 284 285 Men’s Intramurals Mt u’s Intramural is a relaxing way to keep your body in top physical condition and to enjoy yourself at the same time. Competition in sports ranging from basketball to the unique game of squash gives all students the chance to compete for trophies in the intramural program. The Co-rec is kept busy tabulating team standings and presenting awards, bc-sides sponsoring the many athletic events on the agenda. 'lopping the year is the announcement of the winner of the all sports trophy which goes to ilie fraternity having accumulated the most | oints. 28G Women’s Recreational Association 288 Whether it be learning the art of syncronized swimming or just enjoying a rigorous game of basketball, Women's Recreational Association offers a variety of sports to the Purdue coed with only an active interest being a prerequisite for participation. Interfraternity Council Interfratcrnity Council is a body of Greek men who seek to establish better fraternity life for themselves and those who will preserve the system for the future. Functioning as the main governing body for fraternities, it directs rush activities, promotes scholarship, and in co-ordination with the dean of men. handles fraternity disciplinary problems. Other functions of IFC are to do research concerning scholarship. pledging, depledging, and to promote better public relations between the fraternities and the West Lafayette community. 11 MIKEVEON Public Relations STEVE OAKLEY Senior Executive STEVE KOEPPER President PETE EGGLESTON Student Senate Representative PETE FRITH M AN Executive Secretary STAN I EBB . Treasurer The ini|x rtance of pledge class rushing and sc holarship headlined Junior Interfraternity Council discussions. The Council explained the obligations of fraternity members during block rush to their own house and the fraternity system. Emphasis was placed on encouraging the rushee to see many fraternities in order to find the best house for himself. Dirty rush practices were outlined, and the importance of avoiding these methods was stressed. Pledge class scholarship was reported to be rising due to the establishment of house study tables and extensive files. Meredith Governing Board Arriving on campus four days before die Freshmen, the Meredith Hall Governing Board worked out final plans for the orientation program. This session proved to be a great success, and the freshman girls- first week was full of parties and trade dinners. Planning freshman orientation week is but one of the many tasks of the Executive Board. Meeting every Monday night they discuss all the business to be brought before the Governing Board as well as plans for the annual breakfast-in-bed for all Meredith Hall. The Governing Board, working together with the Executive Board, initiated a number of new ideas. As well as setting up a library in their hall, they also instigated a program providing more faculty teas and guest receptions. 387 Duhme Hall Duhme Hall began its social season this year with trade parties with the men from Wiley Hall. TOM McKANE President Purdue’s Finest Hour, Tarkington s display, took first place honors in the all-campus homecoming sign contest. The thirty-foot-high papier-mache and chicken wire figure of Sir Winston Churchill stood in front of Tarkington’s walls to climax the year as has become the tradition. Socially, the year was successful with the memorable Snowflake Fantasy. Si .enter and his band provided the music for the Halberdiers and their dates. Aimed toward the aesthetic development of the men of Tarkington. lectures and debates were presented by the council with topics ranging from dress standards to morals on campus, activism versus passivism. The girls’ homecoming spirit continued throughout the fall as the Court residents participated in trade functions with Courts III and V. intramural volleyball games, and various service projects. Getting into the spirit of Christmas, the girls sponsored an ice-skating party for underprivileged children with the men of Theta Tan. An open house at 1 lallowcen and a formal tea at Thanksgiving were other Court activities. On Mothers’ Day the girls made favors for their mothers and entertained them with a tour of the campus. Fowler Court II “Touchdown! was a familiar cry as Court II engaged in its favorite fall sport, touch football. Sparking the Christmas season were the Court’s annual open house and the formal dance, “Snowflake Fantasy, during which Court II received the G. C. Cobb award for the most uniquely decorated court. Spring weather brought a Court favorite, baseball, and Court H's annual pic nic at Turkey Run. Ending the year, Court scholars were honored at a Scholarship Banquet on May 17 at Morris Bryant's. Miller. •178 Court III started this year with a new slate of officers who immediately picked up the court's activities. A stag party in February, which the majority of the court attended, showed the enthusiasm that the new officers had created. Prizes were awarded for card games, chess, and feats of strength. In the spring a dance, featuring the ''Star-lighter, was held in the center of the court. A new scholarship program was initiated which included a tutoring system and study table, and as a result grades improved. Are you trying to find old school friends, old classmates, fellow servicemen or shipmates? Do you want to see past girlfriends or boyfriends? Relive homecoming, prom, graduation, and other moments on campus captured in yearbook pictures. Revisit your fraternity or sorority and see familiar places. See members of old school clubs and relive old times. Start your search today! Looking for old family members and relatives? Do you want to find pictures of parents or grandparents when they were in school? Want to find out what hairstyle was popular in the 1920s? E-Yearbook.com has a wealth of genealogy information spanning over a century for many schools with full text search. Use our online Genealogy Resource to uncover history quickly! Are you planning a reunion and need assistance? E-Yearbook.com can help you with scanning and providing access to yearbook images for promotional materials and activities. We can provide you with an electronic version of your yearbook that can assist you with reunion planning. E-Yearbook.com will also publish the yearbook images online for people to share and enjoy.
